But I can’t say it’s specifically about how he died. I just know I hear him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month. I mean… roughly since Garrett died, that whole stretch of time has felt like one long crisis, so I’m not sure I can mark the first time neatly on a calendar. But it started after he was gone, and it’s been happening through this past month.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like an isolated thing that happened once and then stopped. It’s more like this whole month has been organized around it somehow—trying to understand why I’m hearing him, trying to convince myself he’s not actually there, and then having it happen again and feeling just as real as before. So… about a month. Since his death.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: When I hear him, it’s… terrifying, honestly, but not only terrifying. That’s what makes it so hard to explain. There’s this immediate jolt, like my whole body believes he’s right there before I’ve even had time to think. If I’m on the trail or in my painting room and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, it’s so clear that I don’t experience it as a memory. It’s not like I’m thinking about him or missing him in an ordinary way. It feels like he’s standing right there.\n\nAnd in that first moment, there’s almost this rush of relief—like, oh, he’s here, I knew it, I’m not alone—and then almost immediately it turns into fear and confusion because another part of me knows he’s dead. I know that sitting here talking to you. But when the voice happens, that knowledge sort of collapses, or gets pushed aside, and I react as if he’s really present.\n\nEmotionally it leaves me shaken. I feel scared, disoriented, desperate to understand whether I’m losing my grip or whether something impossible is happening. And then afterward I feel this heavy grief, because it’s like losing him again once I realize he isn’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're doing or feeling right before you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to find a pattern, because it feels like there should be one, but I can’t honestly say there’s one specific thing I’m always doing or feeling right before it happens.\n\nIt tends to be in places where I’m alone or where there’s enough quiet for my mind to… I don’t know, open up around him. Like on the trail, or in my painting room. Those are the two places that stand out. I’ll be walking, or I’ll be in there with the paints and the light and the silence, and then I hear Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m working myself up to it, exactly. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I miss him,” and then I hear him. Sometimes I’m not consciously thinking anything about him at all, and then suddenly his voice is there, behind me, and my body reacts before I can reason through it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that scares me. Because even though part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it feels completely real. Not like a thought, not like an emotional association. Real. Like if I turned around, he would be right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is coming from a part of yourself, or does it truly feel like it's coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s outside of me. That’s the frightening part.\n\nI can understand why you’re asking, because if it were more like an internal thought, or even a memory of his voice, I think I could maybe place it somewhere inside myself—like grief, or imagination, or my mind replaying him. But that’s not how it lands when it happens. It’s not like I’m “thinking” in Garrett’s voice. It’s not like an inner monologue wearing his tone.\n\nIt sounds like he’s behind me. Like physically behind me. On the trail, or in my painting room, it comes from the space around me, not from inside my head. My body reacts before I can talk myself through it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or feel this rush like someone is actually there.\n\nAnd I know—sitting here, saying it out loud—I know Garrett is dead. Part of me knows that. But in the moment, the voice is so clear and so located outside me that it feels completely real, like if I looked over my shoulder he’d be standing there. That’s why I came in, really. Because I can’t make it feel like just a thought, no matter how much I try afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, smelled, tasted, or felt anything that wasn't actually there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’ve asked myself that, because once the voice started happening I began second-guessing everything—like, am I seeing things too, am I misreading shadows, am I making connections that aren’t there?\n\nBut the reason I came in is really the voice. Garrett’s voice. Hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, usually on the trail or in my painting room. It’s not vague, and it’s not like a smell or a flash or some passing sensation I can dismiss. It feels located in the room, or on the path behind me, and in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nI haven’t noticed smells or tastes or being touched by something that isn’t there. And I don’t think I’ve seen him, exactly. It’s more that the voice is so real that my body expects to see him if I turn around. Part of me knows he’s dead when I’m talking about it now, but when I hear him, that part of me gets drowned out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think hearing Garrett's voice is related to any specific emotions or thoughts you're experiencing at the time?\n\nPatient: I’ve wondered that a lot, because part of me wants there to be a clean explanation, like, “Oh, it only happens when I’m grieving,” or “It happens when I’m stressed,” or when I’m thinking about Garrett too much. That would almost make it feel more manageable, I guess.\n\nBut honestly, I can’t pin it down that neatly. Sometimes I’ll be on the trail, and I’m not consciously thinking about him at all—I’m just walking, looking at the ground, listening to my own breathing—and then suddenly I hear his voice behind me. Or I’ll be in my painting room, and I’m focused on the light or the color or whatever I’m working on, and then there it is. His voice. Clear. Like he’s right there behind me.\n\nI mean, of course there’s emotion after it happens. Fear, mostly. Shock. And then this awful confusion because part of me knows he’s dead, I know that when I’m sitting here talking to you, but in that moment it doesn’t feel like a thought or a memory that came from sadness. It feels real. It feels external. Like the emotion comes because I heard him, not always the other way around.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s something underneath that I’m not catching, but I didn’t come in because I was just having painful thoughts about him. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room, and when it happens I can’t make myself experience it as just an emotion.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the way I’d usually understand “disconnected,” anyway.

When it happens, I feel very much in my body, almost too much. Like my shoulders tighten, my stomach drops, I stop breathing for a second, and everything narrows down to that spot behind me where the voice seems to be. So it’s not like I float away or watch myself from outside, if that’s what you mean. I’m there. I’m painfully there.

But I do feel… shifted, maybe? Like the room or the trail suddenly stops being normal. A second before, I’m just walking or painting, and then once I hear him, it’s like my whole sense of what’s possible changes. I know that sounds odd. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It’s just that my mind can be saying, “Garrett is dead, this can’t be happening,” but my body is reacting as if he’s right there. So in that sense, yes, something feels different—but I don’t know if I’d call it disconnected from myself.

It’s more like I’m split between what I know afterward and what feels completely real in the moment. And the “real in the moment” part wins for a little while.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F44.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I know how that sounds—believe me, I do—but it isn’t like just remembering him or having a thought pop into my head. It’s clear. It’s like he’s standing right behind me, close enough that I almost expect to feel his breath or turn around and see him there.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail sometimes, and also in my painting room. Those are the two places where it’s been the most… vivid, I guess. I’ll be walking or working, and then I hear him say something, and in that moment it feels completely real. Not symbolic, not metaphorical. Real.\n\nAnd the hardest part is that part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it out loud right now. But when I hear him, that knowing kind of gets pushed to the side, and my body reacts like he’s actually there. It scares me, honestly, because I can’t always tell right away what’s real and what’s… not. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: What kind of things does Garrett's voice say when you hear it?\n\nPatient: It’s not always the same thing, which is part of what makes it so unsettling. Sometimes it’s just my name—like, “Hey,” or “turn around,” said in Garrett’s voice so clearly that my whole body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll be on the trail and I’ll actually stop walking because it sounds like he’s right behind me, close enough that if I turned I’d see him standing there.\n\nOther times it feels more conversational, but not in a comforting way exactly. It’ll be something like he’s commenting on what I’m doing in the painting room, or like he’s trying to get my attention. The content isn’t even always the most frightening part—it’s the clarity of it. It’s not like an internal thought with his “voice” attached. It’s outside of me. It sounds placed in the room, or on the trail behind me.\n\nAnd I know—when I’m sitting here, I know Garrett is dead. I know that. But when I hear him, that knowledge sort of drops away for a few seconds, or longer, and it feels completely real. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Can you make Garrett's voice stop when you hear it?\n\nPatient: No. Not really. I mean, I’ve tried—believe me, I’ve tried. I’ll tell myself, very deliberately, “Garrett is dead, this is not real, this is my mind doing something,” and sometimes that helps after a moment, like I can sort of claw my way back to reality. But when I first hear it, it doesn’t feel optional. It’s not like a thought I can redirect or a memory I can push away.\n\nIt’s so clear, like he’s standing behind me on the trail or right there in the painting room, and my body reacts before I can reason with it. I’ll freeze, or turn my head, or stop what I’m doing. And in that moment, even though some part of me knows he’s dead, that part gets drowned out. I can’t just make it stop by deciding I don’t want to hear it.\n\nSometimes if I leave the room or keep walking or focus really hard on something physical, it fades, but I wouldn’t say I can control it. It feels more like I’m waiting for it to pass.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other sensations, like seeing or feeling things that aren't there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voice. That’s the thing that brought me in, honestly—the hearing Garrett as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on the trail or in my painting room. It isn’t vague, and it isn’t like a memory. It feels external, like he’s actually there.\n\nAs far as seeing him or feeling him… I don’t know. I almost expect to, if that makes sense. When I hear him behind me, my body reacts like I’m about to turn around and see him, but the main thing I can really point to is the sound of his voice. That’s the part that feels completely real in the moment, even though I know—sitting here, talking to you—that Garrett is dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen Garrett when you've heard his voice?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve actually seen him. That’s part of what’s so confusing, because when I hear his voice, it’s so clear and so placed somewhere—like behind me on the trail, or in the painting room—that my body is absolutely convinced he’s there. I’ll turn, or I’ll feel like if I turn quickly enough I’m going to catch him standing there.\n\nBut visually, no, I can’t say I’ve seen Garrett. It’s more that hearing him makes his presence feel completely real for those moments. And even though, sitting here, I know he’s dead, when it happens that knowing sort of… falls away. It feels like he’s right there, and then when I look, there’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: How did Garrett die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I know he’s dead, but the details around it are not something I can really give you in a straightforward way right now. It’s like when I try to explain it, everything gets tangled—what I know, what I remember being told, what I’ve replayed in my head, and then what it feels like when I hear him.\n\nI’m sorry. I realize that’s probably not the answer you’re asking for. I just… I don’t feel sure enough to say, “This is exactly how Garrett died,” and I don’t want to make something up or state it wrong. The main thing I know is that he’s gone, and that’s the part my mind keeps crashing into.\n\nDoctor: Is the voice you hear related to the circumstances of Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can tie it directly to the circumstances, exactly. That’s where I get stuck, because the facts of Garrett being dead and the experience of hearing him don’t line up in my head in any normal way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the voice feels connected to him being gone, because I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s right behind me—on the trail, or in my painting room—and in those moments it doesn’t feel symbolic or like I’m remembering him. It feels like he’s actually there. Like if I turned around, he’d be standing there.\n\nAnd then there’s this awful split where some part of me knows he’s dead, I can say that sitting here, but when I hear him, that knowledge just sort of drops out. The voice feels completely real. So… related to his death? Maybe in the sense that it’s Garrett, and Garrett is gone, and my mind keeps making him present anyway. But I can’t say it’s specifically about how he died. I just know I hear him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month. I mean… roughly since Garrett died, that whole stretch of time has felt like one long crisis, so I’m not sure I can mark the first time neatly on a calendar. But it started after he was gone, and it’s been happening through this past month.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like an isolated thing that happened once and then stopped. It’s more like this whole month has been organized around it somehow—trying to understand why I’m hearing him, trying to convince myself he’s not actually there, and then having it happen again and feeling just as real as before. So… about a month. Since his death.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: When I hear him, it’s… terrifying, honestly, but not only terrifying. That’s what makes it so hard to explain. There’s this immediate jolt, like my whole body believes he’s right there before I’ve even had time to think. If I’m on the trail or in my painting room and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, it’s so clear that I don’t experience it as a memory. It’s not like I’m thinking about him or missing him in an ordinary way. It feels like he’s standing right there.\n\nAnd in that first moment, there’s almost this rush of relief—like, oh, he’s here, I knew it, I’m not alone—and then almost immediately it turns into fear and confusion because another part of me knows he’s dead. I know that sitting here talking to you. But when the voice happens, that knowledge sort of collapses, or gets pushed aside, and I react as if he’s really present.\n\nEmotionally it leaves me shaken. I feel scared, disoriented, desperate to understand whether I’m losing my grip or whether something impossible is happening. And then afterward I feel this heavy grief, because it’s like losing him again once I realize he isn’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're doing or feeling right before you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to find a pattern, because it feels like there should be one, but I can’t honestly say there’s one specific thing I’m always doing or feeling right before it happens.\n\nIt tends to be in places where I’m alone or where there’s enough quiet for my mind to… I don’t know, open up around him. Like on the trail, or in my painting room. Those are the two places that stand out. I’ll be walking, or I’ll be in there with the paints and the light and the silence, and then I hear Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m working myself up to it, exactly. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I miss him,” and then I hear him. Sometimes I’m not consciously thinking anything about him at all, and then suddenly his voice is there, behind me, and my body reacts before I can reason through it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that scares me. Because even though part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it feels completely real. Not like a thought, not like an emotional association. Real. Like if I turned around, he would be right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is coming from a part of yourself, or does it truly feel like it's coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s outside of me. That’s the frightening part.\n\nI can understand why you’re asking, because if it were more like an internal thought, or even a memory of his voice, I think I could maybe place it somewhere inside myself—like grief, or imagination, or my mind replaying him. But that’s not how it lands when it happens. It’s not like I’m “thinking” in Garrett’s voice. It’s not like an inner monologue wearing his tone.\n\nIt sounds like he’s behind me. Like physically behind me. On the trail, or in my painting room, it comes from the space around me, not from inside my head. My body reacts before I can talk myself through it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or feel this rush like someone is actually there.\n\nAnd I know—sitting here, saying it out loud—I know Garrett is dead. Part of me knows that. But in the moment, the voice is so clear and so located outside me that it feels completely real, like if I looked over my shoulder he’d be standing there. That’s why I came in, really. Because I can’t make it feel like just a thought, no matter how much I try afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, smelled, tasted, or felt anything that wasn't actually there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’ve asked myself that, because once the voice started happening I began second-guessing everything—like, am I seeing things too, am I misreading shadows, am I making connections that aren’t there?\n\nBut the reason I came in is really the voice. Garrett’s voice. Hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, usually on the trail or in my painting room. It’s not vague, and it’s not like a smell or a flash or some passing sensation I can dismiss. It feels located in the room, or on the path behind me, and in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nI haven’t noticed smells or tastes or being touched by something that isn’t there. And I don’t think I’ve seen him, exactly. It’s more that the voice is so real that my body expects to see him if I turn around. Part of me knows he’s dead when I’m talking about it now, but when I hear him, that part of me gets drowned out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think hearing Garrett's voice is related to any specific emotions or thoughts you're experiencing at the time?\n\nPatient: I’ve wondered that a lot, because part of me wants there to be a clean explanation, like, “Oh, it only happens when I’m grieving,” or “It happens when I’m stressed,” or when I’m thinking about Garrett too much. That would almost make it feel more manageable, I guess.\n\nBut honestly, I can’t pin it down that neatly. Sometimes I’ll be on the trail, and I’m not consciously thinking about him at all—I’m just walking, looking at the ground, listening to my own breathing—and then suddenly I hear his voice behind me. Or I’ll be in my painting room, and I’m focused on the light or the color or whatever I’m working on, and then there it is. His voice. Clear. Like he’s right there behind me.\n\nI mean, of course there’s emotion after it happens. Fear, mostly. Shock. And then this awful confusion because part of me knows he’s dead, I know that when I’m sitting here talking to you, but in that moment it doesn’t feel like a thought or a memory that came from sadness. It feels real. It feels external. Like the emotion comes because I heard him, not always the other way around.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s something underneath that I’m not catching, but I didn’t come in because I was just having painful thoughts about him. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room, and when it happens I can’t make myself experience it as just an emotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a different state or disconnected from yourself when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the way I’d usually understand “disconnected,” anyway.\n\nWhen it happens, I feel very much in my body, almost too much. Like my shoulders tighten, my stomach drops, I stop breathing for a second, and everything narrows down to that spot behind me where the voice seems to be. So it’s not like I float away or watch myself from outside, if that’s what you mean. I’m there. I’m painfully there.\n\nBut I do feel… shifted, maybe? Like the room or the trail suddenly stops being normal. A second before, I’m just walking or painting, and then once I hear him, it’s like my whole sense of what’s possible changes. I know that sounds odd. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It’s just that my mind can be saying, “Garrett is dead, this can’t be happening,” but my body is reacting as if he’s right there. So in that sense, yes, something feels different—but I don’t know if I’d call it disconnected from myself.\n\nIt’s more like I’m split between what I know afterward and what feels completely real in the moment. And the “real in the moment” part wins for a little while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t know how to answer that cleanly.

I mean… Garrett dying was traumatic in the ordinary sense that someone dying can be. It wasn’t ordinary to me, obviously. It was devastating. But if you mean, like, did I witness something violent, or was I physically in danger, or was there some specific horrifying scene that I keep replaying—I don’t know that I can point to something like that. At least not in a clear way.

It’s more that his death feels like this break in the timeline, if that makes sense. There’s before Garrett died and after Garrett died, and I still have trouble talking about it without feeling like I’m stepping into something I don’t fully understand. But I don’t want to invent a dramatic detail just because the word “trauma” is being used. I don’t have some neat memory where I can say, yes, this happened and then everything started.

So… maybe emotionally, yes, it was traumatic. But I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.

It feels located in the room, or on the path behind me, and in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nI haven’t noticed smells or tastes or being touched by something that isn’t there. And I don’t think I’ve seen him, exactly. It’s more that the voice is so real that my body expects to see him if I turn around. Part of me knows he’s dead when I’m talking about it now, but when I hear him, that part of me gets drowned out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think hearing Garrett's voice is related to any specific emotions or thoughts you're experiencing at the time?\n\nPatient: I’ve wondered that a lot, because part of me wants there to be a clean explanation, like, “Oh, it only happens when I’m grieving,” or “It happens when I’m stressed,” or when I’m thinking about Garrett too much. That would almost make it feel more manageable, I guess.\n\nBut honestly, I can’t pin it down that neatly. Sometimes I’ll be on the trail, and I’m not consciously thinking about him at all—I’m just walking, looking at the ground, listening to my own breathing—and then suddenly I hear his voice behind me. Or I’ll be in my painting room, and I’m focused on the light or the color or whatever I’m working on, and then there it is. His voice. Clear. Like he’s right there behind me.\n\nI mean, of course there’s emotion after it happens. Fear, mostly. Shock. And then this awful confusion because part of me knows he’s dead, I know that when I’m sitting here talking to you, but in that moment it doesn’t feel like a thought or a memory that came from sadness. It feels real. It feels external. Like the emotion comes because I heard him, not always the other way around.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s something underneath that I’m not catching, but I didn’t come in because I was just having painful thoughts about him. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room, and when it happens I can’t make myself experience it as just an emotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a different state or disconnected from yourself when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the way I’d usually understand “disconnected,” anyway.\n\nWhen it happens, I feel very much in my body, almost too much. Like my shoulders tighten, my stomach drops, I stop breathing for a second, and everything narrows down to that spot behind me where the voice seems to be. So it’s not like I float away or watch myself from outside, if that’s what you mean. I’m there. I’m painfully there.\n\nBut I do feel… shifted, maybe? Like the room or the trail suddenly stops being normal. A second before, I’m just walking or painting, and then once I hear him, it’s like my whole sense of what’s possible changes. I know that sounds odd. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It’s just that my mind can be saying, “Garrett is dead, this can’t be happening,” but my body is reacting as if he’s right there. So in that sense, yes, something feels different—but I don’t know if I’d call it disconnected from myself.\n\nIt’s more like I’m split between what I know afterward and what feels completely real in the moment. And the “real in the moment” part wins for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you when Garrett died?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean… Garrett dying was traumatic in the ordinary sense that someone dying can be. It wasn’t ordinary to me, obviously. It was devastating. But if you mean, like, did I witness something violent, or was I physically in danger, or was there some specific horrifying scene that I keep replaying—I don’t know that I can point to something like that. At least not in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that his death feels like this break in the timeline, if that makes sense. There’s before Garrett died and after Garrett died, and I still have trouble talking about it without feeling like I’m stepping into something I don’t fully understand. But I don’t want to invent a dramatic detail just because the word “trauma” is being used. I don’t have some neat memory where I can say, yes, this happened and then everything started.\n\nSo… maybe emotionally, yes, it was traumatic. But I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels separate. That’s the part that scares me, honestly.

If it felt like an intrusive thought, or like some part of my own mind talking in Garrett’s voice, I think I could maybe… I don’t know, file it somewhere differently. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I’m generating it. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it or imagining it on purpose. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “What would Garrett say?” and then I hear it internally.

It’s outside of me. Or it feels outside of me. Like behind me on the trail, or behind me in the painting room. That’s why I keep turning around. That’s why it catches me so hard. I hear his voice as clearly as if he’s standing there, and even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it doesn’t feel symbolic or psychological or like a fragment of myself. It feels real.

Afterward, I can sit here and say, “Okay, that can’t be Garrett.” I can understand that. But in the moment, no. It feels like something separate from me, something happening to me.

It’s like he’s standing right behind me, close enough that I almost expect to feel his breath or turn around and see him there.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail sometimes, and also in my painting room. Those are the two places where it’s been the most… vivid, I guess. I’ll be walking or working, and then I hear him say something, and in that moment it feels completely real. Not symbolic, not metaphorical. Real.\n\nAnd the hardest part is that part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it out loud right now. But when I hear him, that knowing kind of gets pushed to the side, and my body reacts like he’s actually there. It scares me, honestly, because I can’t always tell right away what’s real and what’s… not. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: What kind of things does Garrett's voice say when you hear it?\n\nPatient: It’s not always the same thing, which is part of what makes it so unsettling. Sometimes it’s just my name—like, “Hey,” or “turn around,” said in Garrett’s voice so clearly that my whole body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll be on the trail and I’ll actually stop walking because it sounds like he’s right behind me, close enough that if I turned I’d see him standing there.\n\nOther times it feels more conversational, but not in a comforting way exactly. It’ll be something like he’s commenting on what I’m doing in the painting room, or like he’s trying to get my attention. The content isn’t even always the most frightening part—it’s the clarity of it. It’s not like an internal thought with his “voice” attached. It’s outside of me. It sounds placed in the room, or on the trail behind me.\n\nAnd I know—when I’m sitting here, I know Garrett is dead. I know that. But when I hear him, that knowledge sort of drops away for a few seconds, or longer, and it feels completely real. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Can you make Garrett's voice stop when you hear it?\n\nPatient: No. Not really. I mean, I’ve tried—believe me, I’ve tried. I’ll tell myself, very deliberately, “Garrett is dead, this is not real, this is my mind doing something,” and sometimes that helps after a moment, like I can sort of claw my way back to reality. But when I first hear it, it doesn’t feel optional. It’s not like a thought I can redirect or a memory I can push away.\n\nIt’s so clear, like he’s standing behind me on the trail or right there in the painting room, and my body reacts before I can reason with it. I’ll freeze, or turn my head, or stop what I’m doing. And in that moment, even though some part of me knows he’s dead, that part gets drowned out. I can’t just make it stop by deciding I don’t want to hear it.\n\nSometimes if I leave the room or keep walking or focus really hard on something physical, it fades, but I wouldn’t say I can control it. It feels more like I’m waiting for it to pass.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other sensations, like seeing or feeling things that aren't there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voice. That’s the thing that brought me in, honestly—the hearing Garrett as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on the trail or in my painting room. It isn’t vague, and it isn’t like a memory. It feels external, like he’s actually there.\n\nAs far as seeing him or feeling him… I don’t know. I almost expect to, if that makes sense. When I hear him behind me, my body reacts like I’m about to turn around and see him, but the main thing I can really point to is the sound of his voice. That’s the part that feels completely real in the moment, even though I know—sitting here, talking to you—that Garrett is dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen Garrett when you've heard his voice?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve actually seen him. That’s part of what’s so confusing, because when I hear his voice, it’s so clear and so placed somewhere—like behind me on the trail, or in the painting room—that my body is absolutely convinced he’s there. I’ll turn, or I’ll feel like if I turn quickly enough I’m going to catch him standing there.\n\nBut visually, no, I can’t say I’ve seen Garrett. It’s more that hearing him makes his presence feel completely real for those moments. And even though, sitting here, I know he’s dead, when it happens that knowing sort of… falls away. It feels like he’s right there, and then when I look, there’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: How did Garrett die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I know he’s dead, but the details around it are not something I can really give you in a straightforward way right now. It’s like when I try to explain it, everything gets tangled—what I know, what I remember being told, what I’ve replayed in my head, and then what it feels like when I hear him.\n\nI’m sorry. I realize that’s probably not the answer you’re asking for. I just… I don’t feel sure enough to say, “This is exactly how Garrett died,” and I don’t want to make something up or state it wrong. The main thing I know is that he’s gone, and that’s the part my mind keeps crashing into.\n\nDoctor: Is the voice you hear related to the circumstances of Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can tie it directly to the circumstances, exactly. That’s where I get stuck, because the facts of Garrett being dead and the experience of hearing him don’t line up in my head in any normal way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the voice feels connected to him being gone, because I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s right behind me—on the trail, or in my painting room—and in those moments it doesn’t feel symbolic or like I’m remembering him. It feels like he’s actually there. Like if I turned around, he’d be standing there.\n\nAnd then there’s this awful split where some part of me knows he’s dead, I can say that sitting here, but when I hear him, that knowledge just sort of drops out. The voice feels completely real. So… related to his death? Maybe in the sense that it’s Garrett, and Garrett is gone, and my mind keeps making him present anyway. But I can’t say it’s specifically about how he died. I just know I hear him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month. I mean… roughly since Garrett died, that whole stretch of time has felt like one long crisis, so I’m not sure I can mark the first time neatly on a calendar. But it started after he was gone, and it’s been happening through this past month.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like an isolated thing that happened once and then stopped. It’s more like this whole month has been organized around it somehow—trying to understand why I’m hearing him, trying to convince myself he’s not actually there, and then having it happen again and feeling just as real as before. So… about a month. Since his death.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: When I hear him, it’s… terrifying, honestly, but not only terrifying. That’s what makes it so hard to explain. There’s this immediate jolt, like my whole body believes he’s right there before I’ve even had time to think. If I’m on the trail or in my painting room and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, it’s so clear that I don’t experience it as a memory. It’s not like I’m thinking about him or missing him in an ordinary way. It feels like he’s standing right there.\n\nAnd in that first moment, there’s almost this rush of relief—like, oh, he’s here, I knew it, I’m not alone—and then almost immediately it turns into fear and confusion because another part of me knows he’s dead. I know that sitting here talking to you. But when the voice happens, that knowledge sort of collapses, or gets pushed aside, and I react as if he’s really present.\n\nEmotionally it leaves me shaken. I feel scared, disoriented, desperate to understand whether I’m losing my grip or whether something impossible is happening. And then afterward I feel this heavy grief, because it’s like losing him again once I realize he isn’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're doing or feeling right before you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to find a pattern, because it feels like there should be one, but I can’t honestly say there’s one specific thing I’m always doing or feeling right before it happens.\n\nIt tends to be in places where I’m alone or where there’s enough quiet for my mind to… I don’t know, open up around him. Like on the trail, or in my painting room. Those are the two places that stand out. I’ll be walking, or I’ll be in there with the paints and the light and the silence, and then I hear Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m working myself up to it, exactly. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I miss him,” and then I hear him. Sometimes I’m not consciously thinking anything about him at all, and then suddenly his voice is there, behind me, and my body reacts before I can reason through it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that scares me. Because even though part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it feels completely real. Not like a thought, not like an emotional association. Real. Like if I turned around, he would be right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is coming from a part of yourself, or does it truly feel like it's coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s outside of me. That’s the frightening part.\n\nI can understand why you’re asking, because if it were more like an internal thought, or even a memory of his voice, I think I could maybe place it somewhere inside myself—like grief, or imagination, or my mind replaying him. But that’s not how it lands when it happens. It’s not like I’m “thinking” in Garrett’s voice. It’s not like an inner monologue wearing his tone.\n\nIt sounds like he’s behind me. Like physically behind me. On the trail, or in my painting room, it comes from the space around me, not from inside my head. My body reacts before I can talk myself through it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or feel this rush like someone is actually there.\n\nAnd I know—sitting here, saying it out loud—I know Garrett is dead. Part of me knows that. But in the moment, the voice is so clear and so located outside me that it feels completely real, like if I looked over my shoulder he’d be standing there. That’s why I came in, really. Because I can’t make it feel like just a thought, no matter how much I try afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, smelled, tasted, or felt anything that wasn't actually there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’ve asked myself that, because once the voice started happening I began second-guessing everything—like, am I seeing things too, am I misreading shadows, am I making connections that aren’t there?\n\nBut the reason I came in is really the voice. Garrett’s voice. Hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, usually on the trail or in my painting room. It’s not vague, and it’s not like a smell or a flash or some passing sensation I can dismiss. It feels located in the room, or on the path behind me, and in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nI haven’t noticed smells or tastes or being touched by something that isn’t there. And I don’t think I’ve seen him, exactly. It’s more that the voice is so real that my body expects to see him if I turn around. Part of me knows he’s dead when I’m talking about it now, but when I hear him, that part of me gets drowned out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think hearing Garrett's voice is related to any specific emotions or thoughts you're experiencing at the time?\n\nPatient: I’ve wondered that a lot, because part of me wants there to be a clean explanation, like, “Oh, it only happens when I’m grieving,” or “It happens when I’m stressed,” or when I’m thinking about Garrett too much. That would almost make it feel more manageable, I guess.\n\nBut honestly, I can’t pin it down that neatly. Sometimes I’ll be on the trail, and I’m not consciously thinking about him at all—I’m just walking, looking at the ground, listening to my own breathing—and then suddenly I hear his voice behind me. Or I’ll be in my painting room, and I’m focused on the light or the color or whatever I’m working on, and then there it is. His voice. Clear. Like he’s right there behind me.\n\nI mean, of course there’s emotion after it happens. Fear, mostly. Shock. And then this awful confusion because part of me knows he’s dead, I know that when I’m sitting here talking to you, but in that moment it doesn’t feel like a thought or a memory that came from sadness. It feels real. It feels external. Like the emotion comes because I heard him, not always the other way around.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s something underneath that I’m not catching, but I didn’t come in because I was just having painful thoughts about him. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room, and when it happens I can’t make myself experience it as just an emotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a different state or disconnected from yourself when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the way I’d usually understand “disconnected,” anyway.\n\nWhen it happens, I feel very much in my body, almost too much. Like my shoulders tighten, my stomach drops, I stop breathing for a second, and everything narrows down to that spot behind me where the voice seems to be. So it’s not like I float away or watch myself from outside, if that’s what you mean. I’m there. I’m painfully there.\n\nBut I do feel… shifted, maybe? Like the room or the trail suddenly stops being normal. A second before, I’m just walking or painting, and then once I hear him, it’s like my whole sense of what’s possible changes. I know that sounds odd. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It’s just that my mind can be saying, “Garrett is dead, this can’t be happening,” but my body is reacting as if he’s right there. So in that sense, yes, something feels different—but I don’t know if I’d call it disconnected from myself.\n\nIt’s more like I’m split between what I know afterward and what feels completely real in the moment. And the “real in the moment” part wins for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you when Garrett died?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean… Garrett dying was traumatic in the ordinary sense that someone dying can be. It wasn’t ordinary to me, obviously. It was devastating. But if you mean, like, did I witness something violent, or was I physically in danger, or was there some specific horrifying scene that I keep replaying—I don’t know that I can point to something like that. At least not in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that his death feels like this break in the timeline, if that makes sense. There’s before Garrett died and after Garrett died, and I still have trouble talking about it without feeling like I’m stepping into something I don’t fully understand. But I don’t want to invent a dramatic detail just because the word “trauma” is being used. I don’t have some neat memory where I can say, yes, this happened and then everything started.\n\nSo… maybe emotionally, yes, it was traumatic. But I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is a part of you that you're not controlling, or is it something separate from yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels separate. That’s the part that scares me, honestly.\n\nIf it felt like an intrusive thought, or like some part of my own mind talking in Garrett’s voice, I think I could maybe… I don’t know, file it somewhere differently. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I’m generating it. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it or imagining it on purpose. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “What would Garrett say?” and then I hear it internally.\n\nIt’s outside of me. Or it feels outside of me. Like behind me on the trail, or behind me in the painting room. That’s why I keep turning around. That’s why it catches me so hard. I hear his voice as clearly as if he’s standing there, and even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it doesn’t feel symbolic or psychological or like a fragment of myself. It feels real.\n\nAfterward, I can sit here and say, “Okay, that can’t be Garrett.” I can understand that. But in the moment, no. It feels like something separate from me, something happening to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It wasn’t ordinary to me, obviously. It was devastating. But if you mean, like, did I witness something violent, or was I physically in danger, or was there some specific horrifying scene that I keep replaying—I don’t know that I can point to something like that. At least not in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that his death feels like this break in the timeline, if that makes sense. There’s before Garrett died and after Garrett died, and I still have trouble talking about it without feeling like I’m stepping into something I don’t fully understand. But I don’t want to invent a dramatic detail just because the word “trauma” is being used. I don’t have some neat memory where I can say, yes, this happened and then everything started.\n\nSo… maybe emotionally, yes, it was traumatic. But I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is a part of you that you're not controlling, or is it something separate from yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels separate. That’s the part that scares me, honestly.\n\nIf it felt like an intrusive thought, or like some part of my own mind talking in Garrett’s voice, I think I could maybe… I don’t know, file it somewhere differently. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I’m generating it. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it or imagining it on purpose. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “What would Garrett say?” and then I hear it internally.\n\nIt’s outside of me. Or it feels outside of me. Like behind me on the trail, or behind me in the painting room. That’s why I keep turning around. That’s why it catches me so hard. I hear his voice as clearly as if he’s standing there, and even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it doesn’t feel symbolic or psychological or like a fragment of myself. It feels real.\n\nAfterward, I can sit here and say, “Okay, that can’t be Garrett.” I can understand that. But in the moment, no. It feels like something separate from me, something happening to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're reliving a moment from when Garrett was alive when you hear his voice?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 41 / psychologist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me on the trail or in my painting room, and even though part of me knows he’s dead, in the moment it feels completely real.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not exactly. I don’t think I’m reliving a specific moment from when Garrett was alive, like a memory playing over again.

It’s more immediate than that, and stranger. I’ll be on the trail or in my painting room, and I hear his voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me. Not like I’m remembering something he said years ago, or imagining his tone, or getting pulled into some scene from the past. It feels like it’s happening right then. Like he’s there, just out of sight.

And that’s what makes it so hard to explain, because part of me knows—at least afterward, and sometimes even in the moment—that Garrett is dead. I know that. But when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like grief or nostalgia or a flashback. It feels completely real for those moments. My body reacts before I can reason with it. I turn around because I expect him to be there.

So no, I don’t think I’m going back to when he was alive. It’s more like the present gets invaded by his voice.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F44.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice. And I know how that sounds—believe me, I do—but it isn’t like just remembering him or having a thought pop into my head. It’s clear. It’s like he’s standing right behind me, close enough that I almost expect to feel his breath or turn around and see him there.\n\nIt happens when I’m out on the trail sometimes, and also in my painting room. Those are the two places where it’s been the most… vivid, I guess. I’ll be walking or working, and then I hear him say something, and in that moment it feels completely real. Not symbolic, not metaphorical. Real.\n\nAnd the hardest part is that part of me knows Garrett is dead. I know that. I can say it out loud right now. But when I hear him, that knowing kind of gets pushed to the side, and my body reacts like he’s actually there. It scares me, honestly, because I can’t always tell right away what’s real and what’s… not. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: What kind of things does Garrett's voice say when you hear it?\n\nPatient: It’s not always the same thing, which is part of what makes it so unsettling. Sometimes it’s just my name—like, “Hey,” or “turn around,” said in Garrett’s voice so clearly that my whole body reacts before my mind catches up. I’ll be on the trail and I’ll actually stop walking because it sounds like he’s right behind me, close enough that if I turned I’d see him standing there.\n\nOther times it feels more conversational, but not in a comforting way exactly. It’ll be something like he’s commenting on what I’m doing in the painting room, or like he’s trying to get my attention. The content isn’t even always the most frightening part—it’s the clarity of it. It’s not like an internal thought with his “voice” attached. It’s outside of me. It sounds placed in the room, or on the trail behind me.\n\nAnd I know—when I’m sitting here, I know Garrett is dead. I know that. But when I hear him, that knowledge sort of drops away for a few seconds, or longer, and it feels completely real. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Can you make Garrett's voice stop when you hear it?\n\nPatient: No. Not really. I mean, I’ve tried—believe me, I’ve tried. I’ll tell myself, very deliberately, “Garrett is dead, this is not real, this is my mind doing something,” and sometimes that helps after a moment, like I can sort of claw my way back to reality. But when I first hear it, it doesn’t feel optional. It’s not like a thought I can redirect or a memory I can push away.\n\nIt’s so clear, like he’s standing behind me on the trail or right there in the painting room, and my body reacts before I can reason with it. I’ll freeze, or turn my head, or stop what I’m doing. And in that moment, even though some part of me knows he’s dead, that part gets drowned out. I can’t just make it stop by deciding I don’t want to hear it.\n\nSometimes if I leave the room or keep walking or focus really hard on something physical, it fades, but I wouldn’t say I can control it. It feels more like I’m waiting for it to pass.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other sensations, like seeing or feeling things that aren't there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the voice. That’s the thing that brought me in, honestly—the hearing Garrett as clearly as if he’s standing right behind me on the trail or in my painting room. It isn’t vague, and it isn’t like a memory. It feels external, like he’s actually there.\n\nAs far as seeing him or feeling him… I don’t know. I almost expect to, if that makes sense. When I hear him behind me, my body reacts like I’m about to turn around and see him, but the main thing I can really point to is the sound of his voice. That’s the part that feels completely real in the moment, even though I know—sitting here, talking to you—that Garrett is dead.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen Garrett when you've heard his voice?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve actually seen him. That’s part of what’s so confusing, because when I hear his voice, it’s so clear and so placed somewhere—like behind me on the trail, or in the painting room—that my body is absolutely convinced he’s there. I’ll turn, or I’ll feel like if I turn quickly enough I’m going to catch him standing there.\n\nBut visually, no, I can’t say I’ve seen Garrett. It’s more that hearing him makes his presence feel completely real for those moments. And even though, sitting here, I know he’s dead, when it happens that knowing sort of… falls away. It feels like he’s right there, and then when I look, there’s nothing.\n\nDoctor: How did Garrett die?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I can answer that cleanly. I mean, I know he’s dead, but the details around it are not something I can really give you in a straightforward way right now. It’s like when I try to explain it, everything gets tangled—what I know, what I remember being told, what I’ve replayed in my head, and then what it feels like when I hear him.\n\nI’m sorry. I realize that’s probably not the answer you’re asking for. I just… I don’t feel sure enough to say, “This is exactly how Garrett died,” and I don’t want to make something up or state it wrong. The main thing I know is that he’s gone, and that’s the part my mind keeps crashing into.\n\nDoctor: Is the voice you hear related to the circumstances of Garrett's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can tie it directly to the circumstances, exactly. That’s where I get stuck, because the facts of Garrett being dead and the experience of hearing him don’t line up in my head in any normal way.\n\nWhat I can say is that the voice feels connected to him being gone, because I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s right behind me—on the trail, or in my painting room—and in those moments it doesn’t feel symbolic or like I’m remembering him. It feels like he’s actually there. Like if I turned around, he’d be standing there.\n\nAnd then there’s this awful split where some part of me knows he’s dead, I can say that sitting here, but when I hear him, that knowledge just sort of drops out. The voice feels completely real. So… related to his death? Maybe in the sense that it’s Garrett, and Garrett is gone, and my mind keeps making him present anyway. But I can’t say it’s specifically about how he died. I just know I hear him.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month. I mean… roughly since Garrett died, that whole stretch of time has felt like one long crisis, so I’m not sure I can mark the first time neatly on a calendar. But it started after he was gone, and it’s been happening through this past month.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like an isolated thing that happened once and then stopped. It’s more like this whole month has been organized around it somehow—trying to understand why I’m hearing him, trying to convince myself he’s not actually there, and then having it happen again and feeling just as real as before. So… about a month. Since his death.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel emotionally when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: When I hear him, it’s… terrifying, honestly, but not only terrifying. That’s what makes it so hard to explain. There’s this immediate jolt, like my whole body believes he’s right there before I’ve even had time to think. If I’m on the trail or in my painting room and I hear Garrett’s voice behind me, it’s so clear that I don’t experience it as a memory. It’s not like I’m thinking about him or missing him in an ordinary way. It feels like he’s standing right there.\n\nAnd in that first moment, there’s almost this rush of relief—like, oh, he’s here, I knew it, I’m not alone—and then almost immediately it turns into fear and confusion because another part of me knows he’s dead. I know that sitting here talking to you. But when the voice happens, that knowledge sort of collapses, or gets pushed aside, and I react as if he’s really present.\n\nEmotionally it leaves me shaken. I feel scared, disoriented, desperate to understand whether I’m losing my grip or whether something impossible is happening. And then afterward I feel this heavy grief, because it’s like losing him again once I realize he isn’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you're doing or feeling right before you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to find a pattern, because it feels like there should be one, but I can’t honestly say there’s one specific thing I’m always doing or feeling right before it happens.\n\nIt tends to be in places where I’m alone or where there’s enough quiet for my mind to… I don’t know, open up around him. Like on the trail, or in my painting room. Those are the two places that stand out. I’ll be walking, or I’ll be in there with the paints and the light and the silence, and then I hear Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like I’m working myself up to it, exactly. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “I miss him,” and then I hear him. Sometimes I’m not consciously thinking anything about him at all, and then suddenly his voice is there, behind me, and my body reacts before I can reason through it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that scares me. Because even though part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it feels completely real. Not like a thought, not like an emotional association. Real. Like if I turned around, he would be right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is coming from a part of yourself, or does it truly feel like it's coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s outside of me. That’s the frightening part.\n\nI can understand why you’re asking, because if it were more like an internal thought, or even a memory of his voice, I think I could maybe place it somewhere inside myself—like grief, or imagination, or my mind replaying him. But that’s not how it lands when it happens. It’s not like I’m “thinking” in Garrett’s voice. It’s not like an inner monologue wearing his tone.\n\nIt sounds like he’s behind me. Like physically behind me. On the trail, or in my painting room, it comes from the space around me, not from inside my head. My body reacts before I can talk myself through it. I’ll turn, or freeze, or feel this rush like someone is actually there.\n\nAnd I know—sitting here, saying it out loud—I know Garrett is dead. Part of me knows that. But in the moment, the voice is so clear and so located outside me that it feels completely real, like if I looked over my shoulder he’d be standing there. That’s why I came in, really. Because I can’t make it feel like just a thought, no matter how much I try afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, smelled, tasted, or felt anything that wasn't actually there, besides hearing Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean… I’ve asked myself that, because once the voice started happening I began second-guessing everything—like, am I seeing things too, am I misreading shadows, am I making connections that aren’t there?\n\nBut the reason I came in is really the voice. Garrett’s voice. Hearing him as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, usually on the trail or in my painting room. It’s not vague, and it’s not like a smell or a flash or some passing sensation I can dismiss. It feels located in the room, or on the path behind me, and in that moment it feels completely real.\n\nI haven’t noticed smells or tastes or being touched by something that isn’t there. And I don’t think I’ve seen him, exactly. It’s more that the voice is so real that my body expects to see him if I turn around. Part of me knows he’s dead when I’m talking about it now, but when I hear him, that part of me gets drowned out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think hearing Garrett's voice is related to any specific emotions or thoughts you're experiencing at the time?\n\nPatient: I’ve wondered that a lot, because part of me wants there to be a clean explanation, like, “Oh, it only happens when I’m grieving,” or “It happens when I’m stressed,” or when I’m thinking about Garrett too much. That would almost make it feel more manageable, I guess.\n\nBut honestly, I can’t pin it down that neatly. Sometimes I’ll be on the trail, and I’m not consciously thinking about him at all—I’m just walking, looking at the ground, listening to my own breathing—and then suddenly I hear his voice behind me. Or I’ll be in my painting room, and I’m focused on the light or the color or whatever I’m working on, and then there it is. His voice. Clear. Like he’s right there behind me.\n\nI mean, of course there’s emotion after it happens. Fear, mostly. Shock. And then this awful confusion because part of me knows he’s dead, I know that when I’m sitting here talking to you, but in that moment it doesn’t feel like a thought or a memory that came from sadness. It feels real. It feels external. Like the emotion comes because I heard him, not always the other way around.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe there’s something underneath that I’m not catching, but I didn’t come in because I was just having painful thoughts about him. I came in because I keep hearing Garrett’s voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me, especially on the trail or in my painting room, and when it happens I can’t make myself experience it as just an emotion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in a different state or disconnected from yourself when you hear Garrett's voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think so—not in the way I’d usually understand “disconnected,” anyway.\n\nWhen it happens, I feel very much in my body, almost too much. Like my shoulders tighten, my stomach drops, I stop breathing for a second, and everything narrows down to that spot behind me where the voice seems to be. So it’s not like I float away or watch myself from outside, if that’s what you mean. I’m there. I’m painfully there.\n\nBut I do feel… shifted, maybe? Like the room or the trail suddenly stops being normal. A second before, I’m just walking or painting, and then once I hear him, it’s like my whole sense of what’s possible changes. I know that sounds odd. I’m not trying to be dramatic. It’s just that my mind can be saying, “Garrett is dead, this can’t be happening,” but my body is reacting as if he’s right there. So in that sense, yes, something feels different—but I don’t know if I’d call it disconnected from myself.\n\nIt’s more like I’m split between what I know afterward and what feels completely real in the moment. And the “real in the moment” part wins for a little while.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you when Garrett died?\n\nPatient: I don’t know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean… Garrett dying was traumatic in the ordinary sense that someone dying can be. It wasn’t ordinary to me, obviously. It was devastating. But if you mean, like, did I witness something violent, or was I physically in danger, or was there some specific horrifying scene that I keep replaying—I don’t know that I can point to something like that. At least not in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more that his death feels like this break in the timeline, if that makes sense. There’s before Garrett died and after Garrett died, and I still have trouble talking about it without feeling like I’m stepping into something I don’t fully understand. But I don’t want to invent a dramatic detail just because the word “trauma” is being used. I don’t have some neat memory where I can say, yes, this happened and then everything started.\n\nSo… maybe emotionally, yes, it was traumatic. But I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice is a part of you that you're not controlling, or is it something separate from yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels separate. That’s the part that scares me, honestly.\n\nIf it felt like an intrusive thought, or like some part of my own mind talking in Garrett’s voice, I think I could maybe… I don’t know, file it somewhere differently. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I’m generating it. It doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it or imagining it on purpose. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “What would Garrett say?” and then I hear it internally.\n\nIt’s outside of me. Or it feels outside of me. Like behind me on the trail, or behind me in the painting room. That’s why I keep turning around. That’s why it catches me so hard. I hear his voice as clearly as if he’s standing there, and even though some part of me knows he’s dead, in that moment it doesn’t feel symbolic or psychological or like a fragment of myself. It feels real.\n\nAfterward, I can sit here and say, “Okay, that can’t be Garrett.” I can understand that. But in the moment, no. It feels like something separate from me, something happening to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're reliving a moment from when Garrett was alive when you hear his voice?\n\nPatient: Not exactly. I don’t think I’m reliving a specific moment from when Garrett was alive, like a memory playing over again.\n\nIt’s more immediate than that, and stranger. I’ll be on the trail or in my painting room, and I hear his voice as clearly as if he’s standing behind me. Not like I’m remembering something he said years ago, or imagining his tone, or getting pulled into some scene from the past. It feels like it’s happening right then. Like he’s there, just out of sight.\n\nAnd that’s what makes it so hard to explain, because part of me knows—at least afterward, and sometimes even in the moment—that Garrett is dead. I know that. But when I hear him, it doesn’t feel like grief or nostalgia or a flashback. It feels completely real for those moments. My body reacts before I can reason with it. I turn around because I expect him to be there.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m going back to when he was alive.
